<a id="jwt-signing-and-verification"></a>
**JWT Signing and Verification.**


**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - `RS256` algorithm is present but the signing key is committed to source, defeating the control.

⚠ **Anti-pattern:** Secrets hardcoded in source

`RS256`-signed JWTs are issued by `lib/insecurity.ts` on every successful login. The `signToken()` function at line 21 uses an RSA private key defined as a literal constant in the same file, and the `verify()` function at line 189 validates incoming tokens before granting access to protected routes.

The diagram shows the intended JWT issuance flow from login through `token` delivery:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant Login as Login Route (routes/login.ts)
    participant Sec as lib/insecurity.ts
    participant Client as Browser SPA

    User->>Login: POST /rest/user/login with credentials
    Login->>Sec: signToken(user data)
    Sec-->>Login: RS256-signed JWT
    Login-->>Client: JSON response with token and basket id
    Client->>Client: Store token in localStorage
```

**Security assessment**

Two independent weaknesses defeat the JWT signing control:

- The RSA private key at `lib/insecurity.ts:21` is a literal embedded string, readable by anyone with repository access. A clone of the repository is sufficient to sign arbitrary tokens accepted by the live server.
- The verifier at `lib/insecurity.ts:189` passes no `algorithms` option, so the `jsonwebtoken` library trusts whatever algorithm the incoming `token` header names - including `alg:none` (no signature) and HMAC-with-public-key confusion.

Together these two flaws mean the signing key need not even be exfiltrated from the file; any attacker can forge an `admin`-role `token` using the public key as the HMAC secret and the server accepts it.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables arbitrary JWT forgery — The hardcoded RSA private key in source enables offline token signing for any user or role.
- 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Insecure JWT Verification — The verifier accepts attacker-chosen algorithms, nullifying the RS256 key requirement entirely.
- 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — Non-cryptographic RNG for a secret/token — A non-cryptographic RNG at `lib/insecurity.ts:53` weakens any token-generation path that relies on random values.

<a id="password-hashing"></a>
**Password Hashing.**


**Status:** 🟢 Adequate - bcrypt is the configured registration `hash`, but a legacy `MD5` path at `lib/insecurity.ts:41` undermines offline-cracking resistance for affected accounts.

`lib/insecurity.ts`, models/

**Security assessment**

`lib/insecurity.ts` exposes two hashing paths: bcrypt (used at the registration handler) and unsalted `MD5` (present at line 41 for a legacy comparison path). Accounts whose passwords were hashed through the `MD5` path have no effective offline-cracking resistance. The two paths coexist without a documented migration, leaving the bcrypt status claim incomplete. Password storage depth is covered further in [§6.9 Cryptography Secrets and Data Protection](#69-cryptography-secrets-and-data-protection).

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — MD5 used for password hashing allows rapid offline cracking — MD5 hashing at `lib/insecurity.ts:41` produces unsalted single-round hashes recoverable from any dictionary.

<a id="oauth-20-flow"></a>
#### 6.2.1 OAuth 2.0 Flow

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - OAuth implicit flow in use; access tokens exposed in URL fragment.

`oauth.component.ts` reads an access `token` from the Google OAuth redirect URL fragment, calls the Google userinfo API to retrieve the user's email, derives a local password from that email, and calls the standard local login endpoint. There is no server-side OAuth callback; the entire flow runs in the Angular SPA.

The diagram shows how the frontend OAuth adapter terminates in the same local login path as password-based authentication:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as oauth.component.ts
    participant Google as Google UserInfo API
    participant API as Local Login API

    User->>SPA: Return from OAuth redirect with access token in URL fragment
    SPA->>Google: GET userinfo with access token
    Google-->>SPA: Email address
    SPA->>API: POST /rest/user/login with email + derived local password
    API-->>SPA: Local JWT and basket id
```

**Security assessment**

This flow has three compounding weaknesses:

- `login.component.ts:148` uses the implicit flow, which delivers the access `token` in the URL fragment where it is visible to browser history, referrer headers, and any script on the page (🟠 [F-042](#f-042) — OAuth Implicit Flow Without State).
- `oauth.component.ts:30` derives the local password deterministically from the user's email address - any attacker who knows a user's email can compute that password without ever touching OAuth (🟠 [F-040](#f-040) — Predictable OAuth-Derived Password).
- `app.routing.ts:257` exposes the access `token` in the URL fragment on the OAuth return route (🟡 [F-052](#f-052) — OAuth Access Token Exposed in URL Fragment).

The resulting local session then inherits the same SQL-injectable login path and `localStorage` storage described in [§6.2.1](#621-oauth-20-flow) and [§6.3.1](#631-session-token-storage-and-cookie-hardening).

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-040](#f-040) — Predictable OAuth-Derived Password — Predictable derived password allows account takeover without OAuth interaction.
- 🟠 [F-042](#f-042) — OAuth Implicit Flow Without State — Implicit flow exposes the access token in the URL fragment.
- 🟡 [F-052](#f-052) — OAuth Access Token Exposed in URL Fragment — OAuth return route leaks the token into browser history and referrer logs.

<a id="route-authentication-enforcement"></a>
**Route Authentication Enforcement.**


**Status:** 🟠 Weak - Middleware exists but several sensitive routes bypass authentication entirely.

⚠ **Anti-pattern:** Missing server-side authorization layer

`lib/insecurity.ts` exports an `isAuthorized` middleware that reads and verifies the JWT from the `Authorization` header. This middleware is applied to most sensitive routes in `server.ts`. The authenticated user's identity is then available to route handlers via `authenticatedUsers.tokenMap`.

**Security assessment**

`server.ts` registers numerous routes without the `isAuthorized` middleware, leaving them reachable by unauthenticated callers:

- File upload endpoints at `server.ts:309` accept arbitrary files with no authentication.
- The LLM chat endpoint at `server.ts:638` and its coupon tool run without any identity check.
- Data-export, continue-code, and profile-image endpoints are similarly unprotected.

Because the authorization check is applied route-by-route rather than by a centralized deny-by-default policy, newly added routes default to open. The client-side `AdminGuard` in Angular (`app.guard.ts:52`) provides no server-side protection.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-037](#f-037) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware — Multiple sensitive routes registered at `server.ts` without `isAuthorized` middleware.
- 🔴 [F-035](#f-035) — Unauthenticated file upload endpoints allow anonymous content injection — File upload endpoints reachable without authentication.
- 🔴 [F-036](#f-036) — LLM06 Unauthenticated chat endpoint enables coupon tool execution without autho… — LLM chat and coupon tool endpoint reachable without authentication.
- 🟠 [F-001](#f-001) — Client-Side-Only Admin Authorization Guard — Client-side admin guard enforces nothing at the server.

<a id="rate-limiting-on-authentication-endpoints"></a>
**Rate Limiting on Authentication Endpoints.**


**Status:** 🔴 Missing - No rate limiting detected; authentication endpoints are open to brute force.

`routes/login.ts` and the login endpoint registered at `server.ts:596` process password-verification and JWT issuance requests. No middleware applies a per-IP or per-account request ceiling to these routes, nor does the application return HTTP 429 on repeated failures.

**Security assessment**

Without a rate limit, an attacker can submit credential guesses at wire speed against `/rest/user/login`. The `MD5` password hashing ([§6.2.2](#62-identity-and-authentication-controls)) means offline cracking is fast; brute force at the API is an alternative. The same gap exists on the LLM chat endpoint at `server.ts:638`, where unlimited requests translate to unbounded upstream LLM costs. No `express-rate-limit` or equivalent middleware was detected in `server.ts`.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-030](#f-030) — No rate limiting on login endpoint enables credential stuffing and DoS — No rate limiting on the login route in `routes/login.ts`.
- 🟠 [F-032](#f-032) — No rate limiting on login endpoint enables credential stuffing — No rate limiting on the login endpoint registered in `server.ts:596`.
- 🟠 [F-031](#f-031) — LLM10 No rate limiting on chat endpoint enables unbounded LLM cost — No rate limiting on the chat endpoint at `server.ts:638`.

### 6.3 Session and Token Controls

<a id="ctrl-session-and-token-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) refuted - 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables arbitrary JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`), 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Insecure JWT Verification, 🔴 [F-026](#f-026) — Unauthenticated admin configuration endpoints disclose internals (`server.ts:606`), +3 · [tb-2](#tb-2) refuted - 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables arbitrary JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`) · [tb-3](#tb-3) refuted - 🔴 [F-015](#f-015) — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel · [tb-5](#tb-5) unconfirmed - 🔴 [F-046](#f-046) — Container image signing via cosign or attest-build-provenance

**Verdict:** 🔴 Missing

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.3.1 Session Token Storage and Cookie Hardening](#631-session-token-storage-and-cookie-hardening)
- [6.3.2 WebSocket Connection Authentication](#632-websocket-connection-authentication)

**Implemented controls:** frontend/src/app/; `lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts`.

**Assessment:** This application uses a single locally-signed JWT for every authenticated session regardless of the login flow in [§6.2](#62-identity-and-authentication-controls) that established it. The `token` is returned in the JSON response body and stored in browser `localStorage` by the Angular SPA's request interceptor, making it readable by any JavaScript executing on the same origin. The `Socket.IO` real-time channel accepts connections without requiring any `token` or identity proof. Neither storage hardening (HttpOnly cookies, BFF pattern) nor WebSocket authentication exists, leaving the session surface fully exposed to XSS and passive network observation.

<a id="session-token-storage-and-cookie-hardening"></a>
#### 6.3.1 Session Token Storage and Cookie Hardening

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - JWT in `localStorage`; no HttpOnly cookie storage or Secure flag detected.

⚠ **Anti-pattern:** SPA without BFF

frontend/src/app/

`login.component.ts:101` writes the JWT received from `/rest/user/login` to `localStorage` immediately on login. The `request.interceptor.ts` at line 13 reads it back and attaches it as a `Bearer` `token` on every outbound API call. There is no server-side BFF layer that would keep the credential inside an HttpOnly cookie inaccessible to scripts.

**Security assessment**

`localStorage` is accessible to all JavaScript on the origin, including injected XSS payloads. Any of the eight components that call `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` ([§6.7](#67-output-encoding-and-rendering-controls)) represent a path from stored content to session exfiltration. The `token` is also returned in the JSON response body at `routes/login.ts:26` (🟠 [F-023](#f-023) (JWT returned in JSON response body stored in `localStorage`)), so a network observer or a logging middleware that captures response bodies receives the full credential.

Replacing `localStorage` with a BFF-managed `HttpOnly Secure SameSite=Strict` cookie removes the entire XSS-to-exfiltration path without any change to the Angular application's routing logic.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — JWT Bearer Token Stored in localStorage — JWT written to `localStorage` at `login.component.ts:101`, readable by any script on the origin.
- 🟠 [F-023](#f-023) — JWT returned in JSON response body stored in localStorage — Token returned in the JSON response body, exposing it to logging and response interception.

<a id="websocket-connection-authentication"></a>
#### 6.3.2 WebSocket Connection Authentication

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - No per-connection identity check on `Socket.IO`; unauthenticated event submission accepted.

`lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts` registers the `Socket.IO` server on the same HTTP port as the REST API. The browser client in `frontend/src/app/Services/socket-io.service.ts:22` initiates the connection on page load without transmitting any credential. Challenge completion events and scoreboard updates are pushed to all connected sockets indiscriminately.

**Security assessment**

`registerWebsocketEvents.ts:23` and `:33` bind event handlers for challenge completion signals and scoreboard broadcasts. Neither the connection handler nor the individual event handlers verify an authenticated identity before dispatching data. Any unauthenticated browser tab can receive the full set of challenge-solved signals including those intended for other users. The absence of a CORS origin check on the `Socket.IO` server also means connections from arbitrary origins are accepted.

Adding a `middleware` call on the `Socket.IO` server that validates the JWT from the connection handshake `auth` object before accepting events would close this gap without restructuring the existing event model.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-015](#f-015) — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel — `Socket.IO` server accepts connections without any token or identity check.
- 🟠 [F-029](#f-029) — CTF flags broadcast to all unauthenticated sockets — Challenge completion events broadcast to all unauthenticated sockets.
- 🟠 [F-033](#f-033) — Uncontrolled Socket.IO connection intake — No connection-rate limit on `Socket.IO` intake.

The diagram shows the session lifecycle from login through WebSocket connection, illustrating where authentication is present and where it is absent:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular SPA
    participant API as Express REST API
    participant WS
    Note over WS: Socket.IO Server

    alt Current state
        User->>API: POST /rest/user/login
        API-->>SPA: JWT in JSON body
        SPA->>SPA: Write JWT to localStorage
        SPA->>WS: Connect - no credential sent
        WS-->>SPA: Accept connection — broadcast all events
    else After mitigation
        User->>API: POST /rest/user/login
        API-->>SPA: Set-Cookie: token=JWT — HttpOnly — Secure — SameSite=Strict
        SPA->>WS: Connect with auth.token in handshake
        WS->>WS: Verify JWT in middleware — reject unauthenticated connections
        WS-->>SPA: Accept connection — send only user-scoped events
    end
```

### 6.4 Authorization Controls

<a id="ctrl-authorization-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) refuted - 🔴 [F-043](#f-043) — Wallet address accepted without ownership proof (`routes/nftMint.ts:41`) · [tb-5](#tb-5) unconfirmed - 🟠 [F-039](#f-039) — CI/CD Workflow Token Permissions Not Restricted, 🟡 [F-048](#f-048) — GITHUB_TOKEN scope minimization


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-002](#w-002)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.4.1 Centralized Authorization Policy](#641-centralized-authorization-policy)
- [6.4.2 Object-Level Ownership Check](#642-object-level-ownership-check)
- [6.4.3 Management Endpoint Protection](#643-management-endpoint-protection)

**Implemented controls:** `server.ts`; `server.ts`, routes/; `server.ts`.

**Assessment:** Authentication middleware (`isAuthorized`) is applied to most routes, but authorization - verifying what an authenticated identity may do to which object - is largely absent. Object identifiers from request parameters are trusted without owner comparison, privileged fields are accepted from request bodies, and management endpoints expose internal configuration without any identity check. The client-side admin guard in Angular provides no server-side enforcement.

<a id="centralized-authorization-policy"></a>
#### 6.4.1 Centralized Authorization Policy

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - Route-by-route authorization with pervasive gaps; no centralized policy enforcer.

`server.ts` applies `security.isAuthorized` selectively to individual route registrations. `security.appendUserId()` at `server.ts:452` injects the authenticated user's ID into `req.body` from the verified JWT, which is the closest thing to a centralized identity propagation mechanism. No policy engine or decorator enforces authorization across all routes by default.

**Security assessment**

The route-by-route application model means that any new route omits authorization by default. Several high-value routes - file upload, chat, data export, and continue-code apply - are registered in `server.ts` without `isAuthorized`, as documented in [§6.2.4](#62-identity-and-authentication-controls). The `AdminGuard` in `app.guard.ts:52` is an Angular routing guard that has no counterpart on the server; an attacker calling the API directly bypasses it entirely.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-001](#f-001) — Client-Side-Only Admin Authorization Guard — Client-side admin guard at `app.guard.ts:52` provides no server-side protection.
- 🔴 [F-037](#f-037) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware — Routes registered in `server.ts` without authentication or authorization middleware.
- 🔴 [F-038](#f-038) — Free deluxe upgrade by omitting paymentMode bypasses payment — Deluxe upgrade route accepts a free upgrade without verifying payment completion.

<a id="object-level-ownership-check"></a><a id="object-level-ownership-check-bolaidor-prevention"></a>
#### 6.4.2 Object-Level Ownership Check

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - JWT authentication present but per-object ownership not verified against the `token` subject.

`server.ts`, routes/

`security.appendUserId()` injects the authenticated user's ID into `req.body`, making it available to route handlers. Routes that use Sequelize's `findOne({ where: { UserId: req.body.UserId } })` pattern correctly scope queries to the owner. However, many resource routes - addresses, basket items, cards, deliveries, orders, memories, and wallet - pass the request-parameter ID directly to the ORM without checking that it matches the `token`'s user ID.

**Security assessment**

`routes/address.ts:11`, `:18`, and `:29` read, update, and delete address records by the URL parameter `id` without comparing to `req.user.id`. The same pattern appears across `basketItems.ts`, `delivery.ts`, `order.ts`, `payment.ts`, and `wallet.ts`. An authenticated user changes only the numeric ID in the URL to access another user's data. The `security.appendUserId()` mechanism is present but unused on these routes.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Insecure Direct Object Reference — Insecure direct object reference across address, basket, card, delivery, order, and wallet routes.

<a id="management-endpoint-protection"></a>
#### 6.4.3 Management Endpoint Protection

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - Admin information endpoints reachable without authentication.

`server.ts` registers `GET /rest/admin/application-version` and `GET /rest/admin/application-configuration` at lines 606 and related offsets. These endpoints are served from the same public port as all other REST endpoints and are not preceded by `security.isAuthorized` middleware.

**Security assessment**

`/rest/admin/application-configuration` returns the full runtime configuration object including JWT secret references and IP filter settings. Any unauthenticated caller on the internet can retrieve this data. The endpoint is publicly listed in the attack surface as `auth_required: false`. Adding `security.isAuthorized` and an admin-role check before both endpoints closes the exposure without any structural change to the application.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-026](#f-026) — Unauthenticated admin configuration endpoints disclose internals — Unauthenticated admin configuration endpoint at `server.ts:606` exposes JWT and configuration internals.

### 6.5 Query Construction and Data Access Controls

<a id="ctrl-query-construction-and-data-access-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-6](#tb-6) refuted - 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — SQL injection via email string interpolation in login (`routes/login.ts:34`), 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — SQL injection request data interpolated into a SQL string `routes/search.ts:23` · [tb-7](#tb-7) refuted - 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — SQL injection in login query bypasses authentication (`routes/login.ts:34`), 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — SQL injection via email string interpolation in login (`routes/login.ts:34`)


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-001](#w-001)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the section's default mechanism — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.5.1 Database Query Construction](#651-database-query-construction)

**Implemented controls:** Sequelize ORM parameterized queries on most data access paths; `models.sequelize.query()` used selectively on login and search routes.

**Assessment:** Sequelize ORM handles the majority of data access with parameterized model methods, providing correct query construction for product catalog, basket, order, and user model operations. However, the two highest-value access paths - the login credential lookup and the product search - bypass the ORM and call `models.sequelize.query()` with user-supplied strings concatenated directly into the query text. The MarsDB product-review update path passes a user-controlled selector structure without constraining its operator set.

<a id="database-query-construction"></a><a id="query-construction"></a>
#### 6.5.1 Database Query Construction

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - Raw SQL string interpolation on login and search routes; NoSQL operator injection on review update.

Sequelize backs most relational data access via typed model methods that parameterize all values. The login route at `routes/login.ts:34` and the product search route at `routes/search.ts:23` call `models.sequelize.query()` directly, constructing the query string by interpolating `req.body.email` and `req.query.q` respectively without escaping or binding.

**Security assessment**

Two independent raw-SQL sinks exist on the most-trafficked routes:

- `routes/login.ts:34` interpolates `req.body.email` directly into the `WHERE` clause. The value `' OR '1'='1` short-circuits the condition and returns the first row - the seeded admin account - without a valid password.
- `routes/search.ts:23` interpolates `req.query.q` into `LIKE` clauses with no LIMIT. An attacker can extract arbitrary column data via UNION injection and cause memory exhaustion with a crafted search term.

The product-review update path at `routes/chat.ts:149` passes the model's output directly into a MarsDB `$where` selector that accepts JavaScript expressions, providing a third injection path through the LLM output channel.

The vulnerable login lookup is built as a raw SQL string:

```ts
models.sequelize.query(
  `SELECT * FROM Users WHERE email = '${req.body.email}' AND deletedAt IS NULL`,
  { model: UserModel, plain: true }
)
```

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — SQL injection in login query bypasses authentication — SQL injection in the login credential lookup at `routes/login.ts:34`.
- 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — SQL injection via email string interpolation in login — SQL injection via email string interpolation in a second login path.
- 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — SQL injection request data interpolated into a SQL string — SQL injection in the product search route at `routes/search.ts:23`.
- 🔴 [F-022](#f-022) — NoSQL \$where JS injection in chat product reviews tool — NoSQL `$where` JS injection in the chat product-review tool at `routes/chat.ts:149`.

### 6.6 Input Boundary Validation Controls

<a id="ctrl-input-boundary-validation-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) refuted - 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — ZIP Slip path traversal in complaint file upload (`routes/fileUpload.ts:33`) · [tb-2](#tb-2) unconfirmed - 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — SQL injection in login query bypasses authentication (`routes/login.ts:34`) · [tb-5](#tb-5) unconfirmed - 🟡 [F-044](#f-044) — Mutable main action reference executes arbitrary code in CI context, 🟡 [F-049](#f-049) — Third-party GitHub Actions pinned to commit SHA

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.6.1 Validation Approach](#661-validation-approach)

**Implemented controls:** `multer` file-size limit on upload routes; Angular template binding for most rendering contexts; path-resolution logic present in some routes.

**Assessment:** No centralized schema-validation framework enforces type, length, or format constraints at API boundaries. `multer` provides a file-size ceiling on upload endpoints. Individual routes perform ad-hoc checks - some reject missing fields, some do not - but there is no shared middleware that rejects malformed or oversized requests before business logic executes. Path traversal, ZIP extraction, and open-redirect vulnerabilities all originate from absent boundary validation on string inputs that influence filesystem and network operations.

<a id="validation-approach"></a>
#### 6.6.1 Validation Approach

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - No centralized input schema enforcement; individual route-level checks are incomplete.

`multer` is configured with a `limits.fileSize` option on the file-upload endpoints in `server.ts`. Some routes check for required fields before proceeding. No request schema library (e.g. `zod`, `joi`, `express-validator`) enforces types, ranges, or formats across the API.

**Security assessment**

Missing boundary validation drives multiple High-severity findings:

- `routes/dataErasure.ts:104` resolves a filepath from a request parameter without canonical containment, allowing traversal outside the intended erasure directory.
- `routes/fileUpload.ts:33` extracts ZIP archives without checking whether resolved entry paths escape the target directory (ZIP slip).
- `routes/redirect.ts:16` tests whether the destination URL `includes()` an allowlisted string rather than exact-matching, allowing bypass with a subdomain-shaped payload.
- `routes/search.ts:23` applies no result count cap, allowing unbounded query results to exhaust server memory.

A shared validation middleware that checks each route's expected parameter shapes would catch all four categories before they reach business logic.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Password reset accepts a security-question answer instead of an OOB `token`; no `token` entropy or expiry constraint.
- 🟠 [F-018](#f-018) — Path traversal filesystem access from request input — Path traversal in the data-erasure handler at `routes/dataErasure.ts:104`.
- 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — ZIP Slip path traversal in complaint file upload at `routes/fileUpload.ts:33`.
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — Open redirect via substring allowlist bypass at `routes/redirect.ts:16`.
- 🟡 [F-053](#f-053) — Unbounded search query with no LIMIT clause amplifies memory use — Unbounded search query with no LIMIT clause at `routes/search.ts:23`.

### 6.7 Output Encoding and Rendering Controls

**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.7.1 Output Encoding and XSS Prevention](#671-output-encoding-and-xss-prevention)

**Implemented controls:** frontend/src/app/.

**Assessment:** Angular's template engine escapes interpolated values by default, protecting the majority of rendering contexts. However, eight components deliberately bypass this protection by calling `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()`, passing attacker-controlled content to `innerHTML` sinks. These bypasses are intentional vulnerabilities in this pedagogical application, but they demonstrate the structural weakness: the Angular escape is the only cross-site-scripting defense, and it has been removed at the most user-visible rendering points.

<a id="output-encoding-and-xss-prevention"></a>
#### 6.7.1 Output Encoding and XSS Prevention

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - Angular sanitization deliberately bypassed in eight components; XSS is exploitable at runtime.

frontend/src/app/

Angular's template compiler HTML-escapes all bound values by default in the majority of the application's templates. `search-result.component.ts`, `administration.component.ts`, `last-login-ip.component.ts`, `track-result.component.ts`, `about.component.ts`, and `data-export.component.ts` call `this.sanitizer.bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` to mark attacker-controlled content as safe before binding it to `innerHTML`.

**Security assessment**

`search-result.component.ts:143` passes the URL-decoded search query through `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` before rendering it as a heading, making the product search field a stored XSS sink. An attacker who stores a `<script>` payload in a product name or review and then triggers a search for that term can execute arbitrary JavaScript in any victim's browser session. Because the JWT is in `localStorage` ([§6.3.1](#631-session-token-storage-and-cookie-hardening)), the XSS payload can exfiltrate the session `token` with a single `fetch()` call - no interaction beyond the search page visit is required.

This call demonstrates where Angular's default escaping is bypassed:

```ts
this.searchValue = this.sanitizer.bypassSecurityTrustHtml(
  `<img src='assets/public/images/padding/1x1.png'>`
)
```

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — Cross-Site Scripting — Stored and reflected XSS via `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` in `search-result.component.ts:143` and seven other components.

### 6.8 Browser and Cross-Origin Controls

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.8.1 CORS Policy](#681-cors-policy)

**Implemented controls:** `lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts`.

**Assessment:** No `Content-Security-Policy` header is present, leaving the XSS defenses entirely dependent on Angular's template engine (which is bypassed in eight components). `Socket.IO` restricts its WebSocket CORS to `localhost:4200`, providing a narrow origin allowlist for the real-time channel. The REST API CORS policy was not confirmed; Helmet is present but an exhaustive header audit was not performed.

<a id="cors-policy"></a>
#### 6.8.1 CORS Policy

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - `Socket.IO` CORS locked to localhost:4200 dev origin; REST API CORS policy not confirmed.

`lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts` passes `{ cors: { origin: 'http://localhost:4200' } }` to the `Socket.IO` server, restricting WebSocket upgrade requests to the local development origin. The Express REST server uses Helmet for header hardening, which by default sets `X-Content-Type-Options` and related headers but does not set `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` without explicit CORS middleware configuration.

**Security assessment**

The `Socket.IO` CORS restriction to `localhost:4200` is functionally a development-only allowlist that would need to change for any non-local deployment. No `Content-Security-Policy` was detected, meaning the absence of CSP is itself a finding for every XSS-exploitable component. The password-change endpoint accepting GET requests (🟠 [F-017](#f-017) — Password change endpoint uses HTTP GET enabling CSRF (`changePassword.ts:13`)) is a CSRF vulnerability because the session `token` is not stored in a `SameSite` cookie - any cross-origin page can trigger the request with a crafted link.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-017](#f-017) — Password change endpoint uses HTTP GET enabling CSRF — Password-change via HTTP GET enables CSRF from any cross-origin page.

### 6.9 Cryptography Secrets and Data Protection


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-003](#w-003), [W-007](#w-007)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.9.1 Managed Secret Storage](#691-managed-secret-storage)
- [6.9.2 Transport Encryption](#692-transport-encryption)

**Implemented controls:** `lib/insecurity.ts`, `routes/checkKeys.ts`; `server.ts`.

**Assessment:** The application relies on `RS256` for JWT signing and commits both the private RSA key and a cryptocurrency wallet mnemonic directly to the source repository. Password hashing mixes bcrypt (registration) and `MD5` (legacy path), with `MD5` providing no effective offline-cracking resistance. Payment card numbers are stored as unencrypted integers. Transport security relies entirely on an external TLS terminator with no startup enforcement, meaning an unconfigured deployment accepts credentials over plain HTTP.

<a id="managed-secret-storage"></a>
#### 6.9.1 Managed Secret Storage

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - RSA signing key and wallet mnemonic committed to source; both are functionally compromised.

⚠ **Anti-pattern:** Secrets hardcoded in source

`lib/insecurity.ts:21` defines the RSA private key as a string constant. `routes/checkKeys.ts:10–11` hardcodes a cryptocurrency wallet mnemonic. Both values were committed to the public repository and are present in git history. Any process that can read the application source - including a free repository clone - can use the RSA key to sign arbitrary JWTs or use the mnemonic to derive and drain the associated on-chain wallet.

**Security assessment**

Secret material committed to source cannot be rotated simply by changing the code; the previous value remains in git history. For the RSA key this means every JWT ever issued can be verified - and forged - by anyone with a clone of the repository. For the wallet mnemonic, the associated private key and any assets controlled by it must be considered compromised from the moment the commit was first pushed.

Remediation requires removing both values, rotating the RSA key pair and issuing fresh secrets through environment variables or a secret management service, and revoking and replacing the compromised wallet.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables arbitrary JWT forgery — Hardcoded RSA private key at `lib/insecurity.ts:21` enables arbitrary JWT forgery.
- 🔴 [F-028](#f-028) — Hardcoded wallet mnemonic exposes private key material — Hardcoded wallet mnemonic at `routes/checkKeys.ts:10` exposes on-chain asset control.
- 🔴 [F-012](#f-012) — Derived private key enables unauthorized on-chain asset control — Derived private key (computed from the mnemonic) enables unauthorized asset transfers.

<a id="transport-encryption"></a><a id="transport-encryption-tls"></a>
#### 6.9.2 Transport Encryption

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - No in-application TLS; relies on assumed external termination with no startup enforcement.

`server.ts` starts an HTTP listener on port 3000. No `https.createServer()` call or TLS certificate configuration is present in the application code. The application is designed to run behind an external TLS terminator (reverse proxy, load balancer), but no startup check verifies that this terminator is in place.

**Security assessment**

In a deployment where the TLS terminator is absent or misconfigured, credentials submitted to `/rest/user/login` travel in plaintext. The password-change route at `routes/changePassword.ts:14` transmits the old and new passwords as GET query parameters (🟠 [F-024](#f-024) — Passwords transmitted as GET query parameters expose credentials), compounding the exposure: those values appear in access logs and proxy logs even when TLS is present. The application should either terminate TLS itself or perform a startup validation that an upstream TLS proxy is configured before accepting external connections.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-024](#f-024) — Passwords transmitted as GET query parameters expose credentials — Passwords transmitted as GET query parameters, visible in logs regardless of TLS.

### 6.10 File Parser and Outbound Request Controls

<a id="ctrl-file-parser-and-outbound-request-controls"></a>

_No trust boundary in this model depends on this control class._


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-005](#w-005)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the section's default mechanism — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.10.1 File Parser and Outbound Request Handling](#6101-file-parser-and-outbound-request-handling)

**Implemented controls:** `multer` file-size limit; `path.resolve()` call present in data-erasure route; allowlist check present in redirect route.

**Assessment:** File upload and archive extraction lack path containment checks, allowing writes outside the intended target directory. The open-redirect endpoint performs substring matching instead of exact-match allowlist validation. The LLM chat route accepts product image URLs from the model output and may issue server-side fetches without destination validation. `multer` provides a file-size ceiling but not content-type verification or path escaping.

<a id="file-parser-and-outbound-request-handling"></a>
#### 6.10.1 File Parser and Outbound Request Handling

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - Path containment and redirect destination validation both bypassed; ZIP extraction lacks traversal protection.

`server.ts:309` uses `multer` with a `limits.fileSize` option to receive file uploads. `routes/dataErasure.ts:104` calls `path.resolve()` to construct the target path but does not assert that the resolved path starts with the expected base directory. `routes/fileUpload.ts:33` extracts complaint ZIP archives using a library that does not automatically sanitize entry names. `routes/redirect.ts:16` checks `allowedRedirectHosts.some(host => url.includes(host))` to validate the destination.

**Security assessment**

Three weaknesses follow from the same root cause - trusting user-controlled path components without canonical containment:

- `routes/dataErasure.ts:104` resolves the path but accepts a traversal sequence like `../../../../etc/passwd` because `path.resolve()` does not assert containment - it merely canonicalizes.
- `routes/fileUpload.ts:33` writes archive entries to paths derived from entry names; a ZIP with an entry named `../../server.ts` overwrites application source files.
- `routes/redirect.ts:16` uses `includes()`, meaning `https://malicious.example.com/?q=trusted-host.com` passes the allowlist check.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-018](#f-018) — Path traversal filesystem access from request input — Path traversal in the data-erasure handler at `routes/dataErasure.ts:104`.
- 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — ZIP Slip path traversal in complaint file upload — ZIP Slip traversal in complaint file upload at `routes/fileUpload.ts:33`.
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — Open redirect via substring allowlist bypass at `routes/redirect.ts:16`.
- 🟠 [F-025](#f-025) — LLM07 Confidential escalation policy in system prompt extractable via injection — Unauthenticated admin configuration endpoint discloses internals (🔴 [F-026](#f-026) — Unauthenticated admin configuration endpoints disclose internals cross-reference).
- 🟠 [F-029](#f-029) — CTF flags broadcast to all unauthenticated sockets — CTF challenge flags broadcast without per-user scoping on the `Socket.IO` channel.

### 6.11 Operations Runtime and Supply Chain Controls


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-006](#w-006)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Missing

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.11.1 Third-Party Action Pinning](#6111-third-party-action-pinning)
- [6.11.2 npm Dependency Management](#6112-npm-dependency-management)
- [6.11.3 Automated SCA scanning](#6113-automated-sca-scanning)
- [6.11.4 Automated dependency updates](#6114-automated-dependency-updates)
- [6.11.5 Lockfile hygiene](#6115-lockfile-hygiene)

**Implemented controls:** .github/workflows/; `Dockerfile`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`.

**Assessment:** The CI/CD pipeline has meaningful positive controls - `npm ci` and a committed `package-lock.json` give deterministic installs, and CodeQL runs on pull requests. However, several workflows lack explicit `permissions:` blocks, exposing the `GITHUB_TOKEN` to over-broad scope. Three GitHub Actions reference mutable tags instead of immutable commit SHAs, allowing a compromised upstream action to inject arbitrary code into the build. No automated SCA scanning, no automated dependency update bot, and no container image signing are present.

<a id="third-party-action-pinning"></a>
#### 6.11.1 Third-Party Action Pinning

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - Three actions use mutable tag/branch refs; SHA pinning absent for these actions.

.github/workflows/

Most workflows in `.github/workflows/` reference third-party actions by version tag (e.g. `actions/checkout@v4`). `image_actions.yml:33` references at least one action using a mutable `main` branch ref. SHA pinning - where each action reference is a 40-character commit `hash` - is absent for these cases.

**Security assessment**

A mutable tag or branch reference means the action code executed in CI can change without any PR or review. If the upstream repository is compromised or the tag is force-moved, every subsequent CI run executes the attacker's code in a context where `GITHUB_TOKEN` and any repository secrets are available. Pinning to an immutable commit SHA is the only reliable mitigation - tags can be moved, but SHAs cannot be reassigned.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟡 [F-044](#f-044) — Mutable main action reference executes arbitrary code in CI context — Mutable `main` branch reference in `image_actions.yml:33` executes arbitrary code in CI.
- 🟡 [F-049](#f-049) — Third-party GitHub Actions pinned to commit SHA — Third-party actions in `ci.yml` and `codeql-analysis.yml` not pinned to commit SHAs.

<a id="npm-dependency-management"></a>
#### 6.11.2 npm Dependency Management

**Status:** 🟢 Adequate - Lockfiles and `npm ci` in place; automated vulnerability scanning not confirmed.

`Dockerfile`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`

`package-lock.json` is committed to the repository and the `Dockerfile` uses `npm ci` for the production install. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` also uses `npm ci`. The `Dockerfile` passes `--ignore-scripts` to suppress postinstall script execution during the build. These controls together produce deterministic, reproducible dependency installs.

**Security assessment**

The positive controls here - frozen installs and script suppression - are correctly implemented and meet the supply-chain baseline for deterministic builds. The gap is upstream: no SCA tool scans the resolved dependency graph against `current` vulnerability intelligence on each build, meaning known-vulnerable transitive dependencies would not block CI. Renovate and Dependabot are absent, so outdated dependencies are discovered only on manual audit.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟡 [F-047](#f-047) — Untrusted npm Install/Postinstall Scripts Enabled — `npm install` in the base image layer uses `--ignore-scripts` correctly, but postinstall risk remains if scripts are re-enabled by config.
- 🟡 [F-051](#f-051) — `Package-lock.json` present and committed — `package-lock.json` is present; this finding tracks that it stays committed and `npm ci` is enforced.

<a id="automated-sca-scanning"></a>
#### 6.11.3 Automated SCA scanning

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - no dependency vulnerability scanner runs in CI.

No Snyk, OWASP Dependency-Check, `npm audit --audit-level`, or equivalent SCA step was detected in any of the GitHub Actions workflow files. CodeQL provides SAST coverage but does not scan the resolved dependency graph against vulnerability intelligence databases.

**Security assessment**

Without SCA, known-exploitable vulnerabilities in transitive dependencies remain invisible until a developer manually runs `npm audit`. The Juice Shop dependency graph is intentionally large and contains older library versions. Introducing a blocking SCA step in CI - even `npm audit --audit-level=high` as a start - would surface these automatically on every build.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-039](#f-039) — CI/CD Workflow Token Permissions Not Restricted — CI/CD token permissions are over-broad, widening the blast radius of any CI compromise; an SCA scan running in this context would be equally exposed.

<a id="automated-dependency-updates"></a>
#### 6.11.4 Automated dependency updates

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - no Renovate, Dependabot, or equivalent dependency update automation detected.

No `.github/dependabot.yml`, `renovate.json`, or equivalent automated dependency update configuration is present. The `.github/workflows/` directory contains release, linting, and scanning workflows, but `none` that create PRs for dependency updates.

**Security assessment**

Without automation, the dependency update cadence depends entirely on manual developer attention. For a project with intentionally outdated dependencies (as a pedagogical application), this is by design, but for any production fork the absence of update automation means security patches accumulate until a manual audit is performed.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟡 [F-048](#f-048) — GITHUB_TOKEN scope minimization — `GITHUB_TOKEN` scope is not minimized in several workflows, meaning an automated update PR runner would inherit over-broad repository access.

<a id="lockfile-hygiene"></a>
#### 6.11.5 Lockfile hygiene

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - `package-lock.json` is committed but lockfile integrity enforcement in CI is partial.

The `package-lock.json` is committed and `npm ci` is used in the production install paths. However, several CI workflow jobs that run `npm install` steps were not confirmed to use `npm ci` exclusively, leaving the possibility that the lockfile could drift without a blocking check.

**Security assessment**

Lockfile hygiene requires that every install in CI and production uses `npm ci` (which validates against the lockfile and fails on drift) rather than `npm install` (which can update the lockfile silently). Confirming that all install steps use `npm ci` and that a lockfile-changed PR check is enabled closes this gap.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟡 [F-051](#f-051) — `Package-lock.json` present and committed — `package-lock.json` is committed; `npm ci` usage across all workflows is not fully confirmed.

### 6.12 Real-time and Not Applicable Controls

<!-- §6.12 LOCKED — mechanically derived from absence of real-time findings. Renderer must not rewrite the line below. -->
_Not applicable - no real-time / WebSocket findings routed to this category, and no AI/LLM, GraphQL, or gRPC surfaces detected by the recon scan. Controls catalogued elsewhere (container hardening, dependency determinism) are covered in their primary [§6](#6-security-architecture) sections._

### 6.13 Defense-in-Depth Summary

**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe

The positive controls that exist in this codebase are: `RS256` as the JWT signing algorithm (correctly chosen, though the key is committed), `npm ci` and a committed lockfile for deterministic builds, `multer` file-size limits on upload endpoints, Helmet for common HTTP security headers, `--ignore-scripts` on the production `npm install`, CodeQL SAST on pull requests, and a non-root UID (65532) in the `main` `Dockerfile`. These controls form a thin positive baseline - the algorithm choice is sound, the build pipeline has reproducible installs, and the runtime image avoids root execution.

The boundary repairs that would restore meaningful layered defense are: rotating and externalizing the RSA signing key (removes the primary auth-bypass chain), parameterizing the login and search SQL queries (removes the injection chain), enforcing an algorithm allowlist in the JWT verifier (closes the alg-confusion path independently of key secrecy), moving the bearer `token` to an HttpOnly cookie via a BFF (removes the XSS-to-exfiltration path), adding `isAuthorized` middleware to all sensitive routes (establishes a server-side authorization floor), and activating SCA scanning in CI (provides continuous visibility into dependency-chain vulnerabilities). Each repair is independently valuable; the first three collectively dismantle every Critical attack path documented in this assessment.

## 7. Weakness Register

Systemic control gaps behind the findings, ordered by severity (W-001 = most severe). Each weakness names the missing, home-grown, or misused control, the findings that evidence it, the components it spans, and its remediation. A weakness may also rest on observed unsafe practice or an absent architectural control with no confirmed exploit - only confirmed findings carry a CVSS score.

- 🔴 **Critical** · [W-001](#w-001) - Database access relies on concatenated queries · confirmed · 3 findings · 2 components
- 🔴 **Critical** · [W-002](#w-002) - `Authorization` is implemented route by route · confirmed · 4 findings · 1 component
- 🔴 **Critical** · [W-003](#w-003) - Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store · confirmed · 3 findings · 2 components
- 🟠 **High** · [W-004](#w-004) - Endpoints are reachable without enforced authentication · confirmed · 3 findings · 2 components
- 🟠 **High** · [W-005](#w-005) - Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation · confirmed · 2 findings · 1 component
- 🟡 **Medium** · [W-006](#w-006) - Build pipeline trusts mutable third-party references · confirmed · 2 findings · 1 component
- 🟡 **Medium** · [W-007](#w-007) - Security-sensitive data uses weak cryptographic primitives · observed-practice · 2 findings · 2 components
- 🟡 **Medium** · [W-008](#w-008) - Frontend rendering lacks enforced output encoding · design-risk · 0 findings · 0 components

<a id="w-001"></a>
### W-001 — Database access relies on concatenated queries

🔴 **Critical** · design weakness · confirmed · 3 findings

Database queries are assembled from application values instead of passing those values through an enforced parameterised data-access path. This leaves every call site responsible for preserving query structure.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Raw SQL string interpolation.** Login and product search routes bypass the Sequelize ORM and call `models.sequelize.query()` with user-supplied strings concatenated directly into SQL. Fixing individual call sites without replacing the query construction pattern leaves newly added routes at risk.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — SQL injection in login query bypasses authentication (`routes/login.ts:34`)
- 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — SQL injection via email string interpolation in login (`routes/login.ts:34`)
- 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — SQL injection request data interpolated into a SQL string `routes/search.ts:23`

**Architecture evidence:** Parameterized Queries, ORM / Repository Layer

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02), [C-01](#c-01)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — provide one parameterised repository or query-builder path and prohibit application-value interpolation in database queries
- **Tactical** — ● [M-003](#m-003) — Use parameterized database queries, ● [M-006](#m-006) — Use parameterized database queries, ● [M-007](#m-007) — Use parameterized database queries

<a id="w-002"></a>
### W-002 — Authorization is implemented route by route

🔴 **Critical** · design weakness · confirmed · 4 findings

`Authorization` depends on per-handler checks instead of a policy boundary that consistently enforces `role`, ownership, and tenant scope. New routes can bypass protection by omitting a local check.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Missing server-side authorization layer.** `Authorization` guards exist only in the Angular router (`app.guard.ts`) and are absent from the Express routes they are meant to protect. A direct API call bypasses all client-side checks; no server-side policy enforcer intercepts privileged requests.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Insecure Direct Object Reference
- 🔴 [F-026](#f-026) — Unauthenticated admin configuration endpoints disclose internals (`server.ts:606`)
- 🔴 [F-037](#f-037) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware
- 🔴 [F-038](#f-038) — Free deluxe upgrade by omitting paymentMode bypasses payment (`deluxe.ts:43`)

**Architecture evidence:** Centralised AuthZ Policy, Role / Scope Enforcement, Ownership Check, Object-Level Ownership Check, Tenant Scoping

**Affected components:** [C-01](#c-01)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce authorization through a shared server-side policy layer and make ownership and tenant scope mandatory inputs to data access
- **Tactical** — ● [M-008](#m-008) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization, ◕ [M-026](#m-026) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint, ◕ [M-037](#m-037) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint, ◕ [M-038](#m-038) — Enforce correct server-side authorization

<a id="w-003"></a>
### W-003 — Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store

🔴 **Critical** · design weakness · confirmed · 3 findings

Cryptographic keys, credentials, and other high-entropy secrets are embedded as literals in source or `config` rather than resolved at runtime from a managed secret store, so anyone with repository read access obtains reusable signing material and credentials.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Secrets hardcoded in source.** The RSA signing key and a cryptocurrency wallet mnemonic are embedded verbatim in source files committed to the repository. Any reader of the repository - including anyone with a cloned copy - can sign arbitrary tokens or drain the on-chain wallet without accessing a secret store.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables arbitrary JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`)
- 🔴 [F-012](#f-012) — Derived private key enables unauthorized on-chain asset control
- 🔴 [F-028](#f-028) — Hardcoded wallet mnemonic exposes private key material (`routes/checkKeys.ts:10`)

**Architecture evidence:** Managed Secret Store, Runtime Secret Injection

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02), [C-07](#c-07)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — move every secret to a managed secret store or injected environment configuration, rotate the exposed values, and add secret-scanning to CI
- **Tactical** — ● [M-004](#m-004) — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store, ● [M-012](#m-012) — Move secrets to a managed secret store, ◕ [M-028](#m-028) — Move secrets to a managed secret store

<a id="w-004"></a>
### W-004 — Endpoints are reachable without enforced authentication

🟠 **High** · design weakness · confirmed · 3 findings

Sensitive API routes and real-time channels are exposed without an enforced authentication check at the endpoint boundary. Access control depends on each handler (or the caller) remembering to require a session, so an unauthenticated client can reach privileged operations directly.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-015](#f-015) — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel
- 🔴 [F-035](#f-035) — Unauthenticated file upload endpoints allow anonymous content injection
- 🔴 [F-036](#f-036) — LLM06 Unauthenticated chat endpoint enables coupon tool execution without autho…

**Architecture evidence:** Route Authentication Middleware, Server-Side Session Enforcement

**Affected components:** [C-01](#c-01), [C-04](#c-04)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce authentication centrally at the routing and channel boundary so every exposed endpoint requires a verified session unless explicitly marked public
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-015](#m-015) — Require authentication on every exposed endpoint, ◕ [M-035](#m-035) — Require authentication on every exposed endpoint, ◕ [M-036](#m-036) — Require authentication on every exposed endpoint

<a id="w-005"></a>
### W-005 — Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation

🟠 **High** · design weakness · confirmed · 2 findings

Request handlers do not validate input against one enforced server-side schema or allowlist at the boundary - validation is either absent on the vulnerable parameters or limited to rejecting selected bad patterns (a blacklist). New encodings and unanticipated input forms can therefore reach downstream parsers or interpreters.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-018](#f-018) — Path traversal filesystem access from request input `routes/dataErasure.ts:104`
- 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — ZIP Slip path traversal in complaint file upload (`routes/fileUpload.ts:33`)

**Architecture evidence:** Schema Validation, Allowlist Validation

**Affected components:** [C-01](#c-01)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce server-side schemas and domain-specific allowlists before input reaches parsing, persistence, or command construction
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-018](#m-018) — Constrain file paths to a safe base directory, ◕ [M-019](#m-019) — Constrain file paths to a safe base directory

<a id="w-006"></a>
### W-006 — Build pipeline trusts mutable third-party references

🟡 **Medium** · design weakness · confirmed · 2 findings

CI/CD workflows resolve third-party actions and other build dependencies to mutable tags or branches instead of immutable commit digests, so a retagged or compromised upstream runs inside the pipeline with its `token` and secret scope.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟡 [F-044](#f-044) — Mutable main action reference executes arbitrary code in CI context
- 🟡 [F-049](#f-049) — Third-party GitHub Actions pinned to commit SHA

**Architecture evidence:** Commit-SHA Action Pinning, Dependency Provenance Verification

**Affected components:** [C-06](#c-06)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — pin every third-party action and build dependency to an immutable commit SHA (or a vetted internal mirror) and enforce SHA-pinning in CI
- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-044](#m-044) — Pin third-party dependencies to immutable versions, ◑ [M-049](#m-049) — Pin third-party dependencies to immutable versions

<a id="w-007"></a>
### W-007 — Security-sensitive data uses weak cryptographic primitives

🟡 **Medium** · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 2 findings

Password, `token`, or integrity protection uses a weak `hash`, predictable random source, or insufficient work factor. The application may use a standard library, but the selected primitive does not provide the required security property.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — MD5 used for password hashing allows rapid offline cracking (`insecurity.ts:41`) (`lib/insecurity.ts:41`)
- 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — Non-cryptographic RNG for a secret/token `lib/insecurity.ts:53` (`lib/insecurity.ts:53`)

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02), [C-01](#c-01)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — standardise on a password KDF, a CSPRNG for secrets, and modern authenticated cryptographic primitives with centrally reviewed parameters
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-016](#m-016) — Use modern cryptographic hash and KDF algorithms, ◕ [M-021](#m-021) — Use cryptographically secure random values

<a id="w-008"></a>
### W-008 — Frontend rendering lacks enforced output encoding

🟡 **Medium** · design weakness · design-risk

Browser rendering paths write HTML or DOM content directly without one enforced contextual-encoding or sanitisation boundary. Safety depends on each individual component using the right API.

**Architecture evidence:** Template Autoescape, Output Encoding, Content Security Policy

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — use framework-safe rendering by default and isolate any required raw HTML behind one reviewed sanitisation and Trusted Types policy

---

## 7b. Requirements Compliance

The most critical gaps are in authentication and access control: SQL injection bypasses login, JWT verification accepts attacker-chosen algorithms, and no rate limiting covers authentication endpoints. Token storage in browser-accessible `localStorage` violates the session-credential isolation requirement across the entire user base. The LLM chat surface fails every applicable AI security requirement - prompt injection is unmitigated, tool actions require no authorization, and the endpoint is reachable without authentication.

<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/ai-llm-security#llm-001"><code>LLM-001</code></a>: Prompts and retrieved content treated as untrusted; prompt injection mitigated</td><td>❌ FAIL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">🟠 <a href="#f-020">F-020</a> — user messages are concatenated into the LLM prompt at <code>routes/chat.ts:191</code> with no role separation, filtering, or injection mitigation.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/ai-llm-security#llm-002"><code>LLM-002</code></a>: Context data authorized and minimized before model invocation</td><td>❌ FAIL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">🟠 <a href="#f-025">F-025</a> — the system prompt at <code>routes/chat.ts:105</code> contains the application's coupon escalation policy; no per-tenant or per-user context authorization is enforced.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/ai-llm-security#llm-005"><code>LLM-005</code></a>: Model output validated against schema before use in downstream sinks</td><td>❌ FAIL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">🔴 <a href="#f-022">F-022</a> — product-review tool at <code>routes/chat.ts:149</code> passes model-derived output directly into a MarsDB <code>$where</code> JS selector.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/ai-llm-security#llm-006"><code>LLM-006</code></a>: Tool actions authorized server-side; high-impact actions require approval</td><td>❌ FAIL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">🔴 <a href="#f-036">F-036</a> — the chat endpoint and its coupon tool are reachable without authentication; no server-side authorization check guards tool invocations.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/ai-llm-security#llm-007"><code>LLM-007</code></a>: System prompt contains no credentials, secrets, or hidden authorization rules</td><td>❌ FAIL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">🟠 <a href="#f-025">F-025</a> — coupon escalation policy is embedded in the system prompt rather than enforced by deterministic server-side code.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/ai-llm-security#llm-010"><code>LLM-010</code></a>: LLM features bound by input size, rate, cost, and concurrency limits</td><td>❌ FAIL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">🟠 <a href="#f-031">F-031</a> — no rate limiting, token budget, or concurrency cap is applied to the chat endpoint.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/web-frontend-security#web-003"><code>WEB-003</code></a>: CORS restricted to exact-match trusted origins</td><td>⚠️ PARTIAL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><code>Socket.IO</code> CORS is locked to <code>localhost:4200</code>; REST API CORS policy is not confirmed; no wildcard found but exact-match enforcement for REST is unverifiable.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/web-frontend-security#web-005"><code>WEB-005</code></a>: HSTS with 1-year max-age</td><td>⚠️ PARTIAL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Application does not terminate TLS internally; HSTS depends on an upstream terminator whose configuration is not in scope.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/web-frontend-security#web-006"><code>WEB-006</code></a>: Frontend dependencies version-locked and integrity-verified</td><td>⚠️ PARTIAL</td><td>SHOULD</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><code>package-lock.json</code> is committed and <code>npm ci</code> is used in CI, but no integrity pinning beyond the lockfile is present.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/web-frontend-security#web-008"><code>WEB-008</code></a>: <code>X-Content-Type-Options</code>, Referrer-Policy, Permissions-Policy, framing restriction</td><td>⚠️ PARTIAL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Helmet is loaded but a complete header audit was not confirmed; <code>X-Frame-Options</code> and <code>Referrer-Policy</code> presence is unverified.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/error-handling-security-events#eh-001"><code>EH-001</code></a>: Errors leave application in secure state; fail closed</td><td>⚠️ PARTIAL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Several routes lack authentication middleware entirely (🔴 <a href="#f-037">F-037</a> — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware (<code>server.ts:310</code>)), meaning failures on those routes never reach an authorization check.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/system-hardening#hn-001"><code>HN-001</code></a>: No unnecessary technology-disclosure headers</td><td>⚠️ PARTIAL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Helmet is loaded at <code>server.ts</code>; exact header suppression list was not confirmed for all default Express headers.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/authentication-access-control#ac-008"><code>AC-008</code></a>: OOB codes single-use, short-lived, no full session before verification</td><td>⚠️ PARTIAL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">🟠 <a href="#f-013">F-013</a> — password reset accepts a security-question answer rather than an OOB token, missing single-use and short-lived guarantees; TOTP setup flow was not fully traced.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/data-protection-cryptography#dp-006"><code>DP-006</code></a>: TLS 1.2+ on all off-loopback traffic</td><td>⚠️ PARTIAL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Application listens on plain HTTP and relies on an external TLS terminator; TLS presence is assumed but not enforced at startup.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/supply-chain-dependency-security#sc-006"><code>SC-006</code></a>: SAST in CI blocks exploitable findings</td><td>⚠️ PARTIAL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">CodeQL is configured in <code>.github/workflows/codeql-analysis.yml</code> but the block-on-finding policy and suppression governance are not confirmed.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/infrastructure-container-security#if-002"><code>IF-002</code></a>: Container runs as non-root numeric identity; no privilege escalation</td><td>⚠️ PARTIAL</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Main <code>Dockerfile</code> sets UID 65532; 🟡 <a href="#f-045">F-045</a> — smoke-test <code>Dockerfile</code> runs as root; deployment policy enforcement was not confirmed.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/error-handling-security-events#eh-003"><code>EH-003</code></a>: Non-enumerating auth responses</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Login error messages were not directly audited for user-enumeration behavior in the static analysis scope.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/authentication-access-control#ac-001"><code>AC-001</code></a>: Service-to-service calls use dedicated workload identity</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Outbound LLM calls to Ollama/OpenAI provider are the only service-to-service flow; authentication configuration for that provider was not confirmed.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/authentication-access-control#ac-007"><code>AC-007</code></a>: Session IDs rotated on auth/privilege change; server-side invalidation</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">JWT is stateless with no server-side revocation store; whether expiry and rotation are enforced on privilege change is not verifiable from static analysis.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/data-protection-cryptography#dp-007"><code>DP-007</code></a>: TLS certificate chain validated; no production bypass</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">TLS client configuration for outbound LLM calls was not confirmed from static analysis.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/data-protection-cryptography#dp-008"><code>DP-008</code></a>: Data minimization, documented collection purpose, verifiable deletion</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Data handling documentation and deletion behavior were not assessed in this review scope.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/supply-chain-dependency-security#sc-003"><code>SC-003</code></a>: Trusted registries allowlisted; private sources authenticated</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Registry allowlist configuration was not found in the CI or <code>Dockerfile</code> scope.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/security-logging-monitoring#lm-003"><code>LM-003</code></a>: Centralized tamper-evident log storage</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">No log forwarding or centralized SIEM integration was detected in the codebase.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/security-logging-monitoring#lm-004"><code>LM-004</code></a>: Documented retention and deletion policy</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>SHOULD</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">No retention policy documentation was found in scope.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/security-logging-monitoring#lm-005"><code>LM-005</code></a>: Alerting on high-confidence security events</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>SHOULD</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">No alerting integration detected; dependent on <a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/security-logging-monitoring#lm-003"><code>LM-003</code></a>.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/security-logging-monitoring#lm-006"><code>LM-006</code></a>: No passwords, tokens, or keys in logs</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Log output content was not fully traced; no explicit log masking was detected.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/infrastructure-container-security#if-003"><code>IF-003</code></a>: Read-only root filesystem</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>SHOULD</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Container filesystem configuration is not specified in the Dockerfiles.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/infrastructure-container-security#if-004"><code>IF-004</code></a>: All capabilities dropped; restrictive seccomp</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">No capability drop or seccomp profile was found in the Dockerfiles or deployment manifests.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/infrastructure-container-security#if-005"><code>IF-005</code></a>: Default-deny ingress/egress network policy</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">No Kubernetes network policy or equivalent was found in scope.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/infrastructure-container-security#if-008"><code>IF-008</code></a>: Infrastructure changes from version-controlled reviewed definitions</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>MUST</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">No separate infrastructure code repository or change workflow is in scope.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/ai-llm-security#llm-003"><code>LLM-003</code></a>: Model and SDK versions pinned and integrity-verified</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>SHOULD</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Model provider and SDK version pinning were not confirmed from static analysis.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a href="https://appsec.int.example.com/req/ai-llm-security#llm-009"><code>LLM-009</code></a>: Model output grounded in authorized sources; human review for consequential decisions</td><td>❓ UNVERIFIABLE</td><td>SHOULD</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Chat output use cases were not fully mapped for consequential decision paths.</td></tr>

</table>

### Requirement Scope

- Source: [Curated OWASP-informed Demo Requirements](https://appsec.int.example.com/req); 73 requirements in 11 categories, plus 12 blueprint guidance entries.
- Policy context: no organization profile is active; this is a generic baseline assessment, not a project-specific compliance attestation.
- Compliance scope: no explicit `compliance_scope` was configured; applicability is inferred from repository evidence.
- Traceability rule: only FAIL, PARTIAL and ANTI-PATTERN rows are treated as violated requirements; PASS, N/A, NOT OBSERVABLE and UNVERIFIABLE rows are excluded from violation traceability.
- Blueprint entries are implementation guidance only; they do not add requirements or change PASS/FAIL counts.

### Requirements Traceability

Deterministic mapping of each FAIL, PARTIAL or ANTI-PATTERN requirement to the findings that evidence it and the mitigations that remediate it. PASS, N/A, NOT OBSERVABLE and UNVERIFIABLE requirements are deliberately excluded. Guidance links are non-normative blueprint references, not additional requirements.

## 8. Findings Register

Findings are grouped by severity (Critical → High → Medium → Low); within a tier they are ordered by attack vektor (Repo-Read → Internet-Anon → Internet-User → Victim-Required). Each finding is a card with the same fixed fields, in order: **Severity · Component · Location** → **Issue** → **Root cause** → **Evidence** → **Fix** → **Classification** (with external CWE / OWASP links).

**Risk Distribution:** 🔴 Critical: 10 · 🟠 High: 29 · 🟡 Medium: 14 · 🟢 Low: n/a · **Total findings: 53**
**STRIDE Coverage:** Spoofing: 9 · Tampering: 12 · Repudiation: 1 · Information Disclosure: 16 · Denial of Service: 5 · Elevation of Privilege: 10

The systemic root-cause view is summarized in **Top Weaknesses** in the Management Summary; evidence-backed weaknesses are documented in the [Weakness Register](#weakness-register).
